```java id="iz8nqv"
sslContext.init(null, tmf.getTrustManagers(), new SecureRandom());

HttpClient client = HttpClient.newBuilder()
    .sslContext(sslContext)
    .build();
```

Rule:

```text id="bsn94n"
Do not use trust-all TrustManager in production.
Do not disable hostname verification.
Do not ignore SSLHandshakeException.
```

---

# 17. Secrets Handling

## 17.1 Bad

```java id="9ngk5d"
private static final String API_KEY = "secret-value";
```

## 17.2 Better

```java id="84wyvo"
String apiKey = System.getenv("API_KEY");

if (apiKey == null || apiKey.isBlank()) {
    throw new IllegalStateException("API_KEY is required");
}
```

Better still in production:

```text id="n4aaxj"
secret manager
KMS
Vault
Kubernetes Secret with strict RBAC
cloud secret manager
hardware-backed key storage where required
```

---

## 17.3 Do Not Log Secrets

Never log:

```text id="xv1q03"
password
private key
secret key
API key
access token
refresh token
Authorization header
Cookie
full encrypted key material
database credentials
```

Mask:

```java id="hnwxig"
public static String maskSecret(String value) {
    if (value == null || value.length() <= 4) {
        return "****";
    }

    return value.substring(0, 2)
        + "****"
        + value.substring(value.length() - 2);
}
```

---

# 18. Common Algorithms

## 18.1 Preferred Default Choices

| Purpose | Recommended Practical Choice |
|---|---|
| Random | `SecureRandom` |
| Symmetric encryption | `AES/GCM/NoPadding` |
| HMAC | `HmacSHA256` or `HmacSHA512` |
| Hash | `SHA-256` or `SHA-512` |
| Password hashing | Argon2id / bcrypt / PBKDF2 |
| Password KDF in Java SE | `PBKDF2WithHmacSHA256` |
| Asymmetric encryption | RSA-OAEP |
| Digital signature | Ed25519, ECDSA, RSA-PSS depending ecosystem |
| KeyStore | `PKCS12` |
| Encoding | Base64 / Hex |

## 18.2 Avoid for New Designs

| Avoid | Reason |
|---|---|
| MD5 | broken for collision resistance |
| SHA-1 | not suitable for new collision-resistant security |
| DES / 3DES | obsolete/legacy |
| RC4 | obsolete |
| AES/ECB | leaks patterns |
| AES/CBC without MAC | malleability risk |
| RSA raw/no padding | insecure |
| RSA PKCS#1 v1.5 encryption | legacy, prefer OAEP |
| `Random` for secrets | predictable/non-crypto |
| hardcoded keys | secret exposure |
| nonce reuse in GCM | catastrophic risk |

---

# 19. AES-GCM Storage Format

Example JSON-ish envelope:

```json id="ysq7xj"
{
  "version": 1,
  "algorithm": "AES/GCM/NoPadding",
  "keyId": "orders-data-key-2026-07",
  "nonce": "base64...",
  "ciphertext": "base64..."
}
```

Store:

```text id="nzqsm4"
algorithm
version
keyId
nonce/IV
ciphertext+tag
optional AAD context
KDF parameters if password-derived
```

Do not store:

```text id="9qvt95"
raw key
password
private key
unmasked secret
```

---

# 20. Associated Authenticated Data

AAD is not encrypted, but it is authenticated.

```java id="m7ex1a"
byte[] aad = "tenantId=t-123;purpose=order-export"
    .getBytes(StandardCharsets.UTF_8);

cipher.updateAAD(aad);
```

Use AAD for:

```text id="53xcdo"
tenant id
record id
schema version
purpose
algorithm metadata
external context that must not be tampered
```

Rule:

```text id="pz2b8h"
AAD during decrypt must match AAD during encrypt exactly.
```

---

# 21. JWT / Token Note

Java SE standard library does not include a full JWT library.

For JWT:

```text id="yp9rxd"
Use a mature JWT library.
Validate:
- signature
- alg allowlist
- issuer
- audience
- expiration
- not-before
- key id
- clock skew
```

Anti-pattern:

```text id="m1a3et"
decode JWT payload and trust it without verifying signature.
accept alg=none.
trust token from wrong issuer/audience.
```

---

# 22. Key Rotation Pattern

```text id="i5qg7a"
Key Rotation
1. Every key has keyId.
2. New encryption uses active key.
3. Decryption supports active + previous keys.
4. Old data is re-encrypted gradually if needed.
5. Retired keys are disabled after retention window.
6. Destroy keys according to policy.
```

Envelope:

```java id="3hjdxx"
public record CryptoEnvelope(
    int version,
    String keyId,
    String algorithm,
    String nonceBase64,
    String ciphertextBase64
) {}
```

Decrypt by key id:

```java id="k69rjh"
SecretKey key = keyRepository.findByKeyId(envelope.keyId());

byte[] plaintext = decryptAesGcm(key, encrypted, aad);
```

---

# 23. Envelope Encryption Pattern

```text id="md95bx"
Envelope Encryption
1. Generate random data encryption key.
2. Encrypt data using data key.
3. Encrypt data key using master key / KMS.
4. Store encrypted data key with ciphertext.
5. Decrypt data key only when needed.
```

Use for:

```text id="99mgfs"
large files
database field encryption
multi-tenant data encryption
cloud KMS integration
per-record encryption
```

---

# 24. Crypto Exception Handling

Bad:

```java id="hvdpj7"
catch (Exception e) {
    return null;
}
```

Better:

```java id="9vozpn"
try {
    return decryptAesGcm(key, encrypted, aad);
} catch (AEADBadTagException e) {
    throw new SecurityException("Ciphertext authentication failed", e);
} catch (GeneralSecurityException e) {
    throw new CryptoOperationException("Decryption failed", e);
}
```

Rule:

```text id="sl926y"
Do not reveal detailed crypto failure reason to untrusted users.
Do log safely for operators without leaking secrets.
```

---

# 25. Testing Crypto Code

## 25.1 Round Trip

```java id="g7ve7y"
@Test
void aesGcmRoundTrip() throws Exception {
    SecretKey key = generateAes256Key();

    byte[] plaintext = "hello".getBytes(StandardCharsets.UTF_8);
    byte[] aad = "context".getBytes(StandardCharsets.UTF_8);

    AesGcmCiphertext encrypted = encryptAesGcm(key, plaintext, aad);

    byte[] decrypted = decryptAesGcm(key, encrypted, aad);

    assertArrayEquals(plaintext, decrypted);
}
```

## 25.2 Tamper Test

```java id="u3rwy8"
@Test
void aesGcmDetectsTampering() throws Exception {
    SecretKey key = generateAes256Key();

    AesGcmCiphertext encrypted = encryptAesGcm(
        key,
        "hello".getBytes(StandardCharsets.UTF_8),
        null
    );

    byte[] ciphertext = Base64.getDecoder()
        .decode(encrypted.ciphertextBase64());

    ciphertext[0] ^= 1;

    AesGcmCiphertext tampered = new AesGcmCiphertext(
        encrypted.algorithm(),
        encrypted.nonceBase64(),
        Base64.getEncoder().encodeToString(ciphertext)
    );

    assertThrows(
        GeneralSecurityException.class,
        () -> decryptAesGcm(key, tampered, null)
    );
}
```

## 25.3 Password Verify

```java id="k1r5hn"
@Test
void passwordVerificationWorks() throws Exception {
    char[] password = "correct horse battery staple".toCharArray();

    PasswordHash hash = hashPassword(password);

    assertTrue(verifyPassword(password, hash));
    assertFalse(verifyPassword("wrong".toCharArray(), hash));
}
```

---

# 26. Production Checklist

```text id="pwzlf3"
Randomness
[ ] SecureRandom used for keys, salts, tokens, nonces?
[ ] Nonce/IV size correct for algorithm?
[ ] AES-GCM nonce never reused with same key?

Hashing
[ ] SHA-256/SHA-512 used for digest?
[ ] Passwords not hashed with plain SHA/MD5?
[ ] Constant-time comparison for secret tags/tokens?

Password Storage
[ ] Argon2id/bcrypt/PBKDF2 used?
[ ] Unique salt per password?
[ ] Parameters stored with hash?
[ ] Rehash strategy exists?

Encryption
[ ] AES-GCM or another AEAD used?
[ ] AAD used where context integrity matters?
[ ] Key id and algorithm version stored?
[ ] Decrypt failure handled safely?

Keys
[ ] Keys generated with secure APIs?
[ ] Keys not hardcoded?
[ ] Keys not logged?
[ ] Key rotation plan exists?
[ ] KeyStore/KMS/secret manager used?

Asymmetric Crypto
[ ] RSA-OAEP used for encryption?
[ ] Large data encrypted with hybrid encryption?
[ ] Digital signatures used instead of HMAC when verifier must not know secret?

TLS
[ ] Certificate validation enabled?
[ ] Hostname verification enabled?
[ ] No trust-all manager?
[ ] Keystore/truststore managed securely?

Operations
[ ] Secrets masked in logs?
[ ] Access to keys audited?
[ ] Crypto errors monitored?
[ ] Test includes tamper cases?
```

---

# 27. Common Anti-Patterns

| Anti-pattern | Problem |
|---|---|
| `new Random()` for token/key | predictable |
| AES/ECB | leaks plaintext patterns |
| AES/CBC without MAC | tampering risk |
| Reusing AES-GCM nonce | catastrophic confidentiality/integrity failure |
| Plain SHA-256 password hash | fast offline cracking |
| Hardcoded key in source | secret leak |
| Logging keys/tokens | credential leak |
| Trust-all TLS | MITM risk |
| Ignoring `AEADBadTagException` | tampering ignored |
| Comparing MAC with `String.equals` | timing risk |
| RSA encrypting large payload | wrong primitive |
| No key rotation | long-term blast radius |
| No algorithm/version metadata | migration pain |
| Custom crypto algorithm | almost always unsafe |
| Parsing PEM manually with ad-hoc string hacks | fragile/security risk |

---

# 28. Practical Rules

```text id="wwn82g"
1. Do not design your own crypto.
2. Use standard algorithms and standard transformations.
3. Use SecureRandom for all secrets/nonces/salts.
4. Use AES-GCM for symmetric encryption.
5. Never reuse GCM nonce with same key.
6. Use HMAC for shared-secret integrity.
7. Use digital signatures for public verification.
8. Use password hashing algorithms for passwords.
9. Use PBKDF2 only when constrained to Java standard library or compliance.
10. Store algorithm, version, salt/nonce, keyId, and parameters.
11. Keep keys out of source code.
12. Prefer KMS/secret manager/keystore for key storage.
13. Use constant-time comparison for secret material.
14. Test tampering and wrong-key cases.
15. Treat crypto failure as security-relevant.
```

---

# 29. Compact Summary

```text id="4jd8os"
SecureRandom
- crypto-safe random
- tokens, salts, nonces, keys

MessageDigest
- SHA-256/SHA-512 digest
- not password storage

Password Hashing
- Argon2id/bcrypt/PBKDF2
- unique salt
- slow and parameterized

Mac / HMAC
- integrity/authentication with shared secret
- use HmacSHA256/HmacSHA512

Cipher
- encryption/decryption
- prefer AES/GCM/NoPadding

AES-GCM
- authenticated encryption
- random unique nonce
- ciphertext includes auth tag
- supports AAD

RSA-OAEP
- asymmetric encryption for small data/key wrapping
- use hybrid encryption for real payloads

Signature
- private key signs
- public key verifies
- authentication and integrity

KeyStore
- stores keys/certificates
- prefer PKCS12

TLS
- keep certificate and hostname verification enabled

Key Management
- generate securely
- store securely
- rotate
- audit
- never log secrets
```

Kesimpulan praktis: **Java cryptography yang aman adalah kombinasi antara API yang benar dan disiplin operasional.** `AES/GCM/NoPadding`, `SecureRandom`, `HmacSHA256`, password hashing yang slow, `Signature`, `KeyStore`, dan TLS validation hanya aman bila dipakai dengan **nonce unik, key lifecycle jelas, secret tidak bocor, parameter tersimpan, dan failure ditangani sebagai security event**.

---

**Sources:**

- [Java Cryptography Architecture (JCA) Reference Guide](https://docs.oracle.com/en/java/javase/24/security/java-cryptography-architecture-jca-reference-guide.html?utm_source=chatgpt.com)
- [Password Storage - OWASP Cheat Sheet Series](https://cheatsheetseries.owasp.org/cheatsheets/Password_Storage_Cheat_Sheet.html?utm_source=chatgpt.com)
- [SP 800-38D, Recommendation for Block Cipher ... - NIST CSRC](https://csrc.nist.gov/pubs/sp/800/38/d/final?utm_source=chatgpt.com)
- [Uses of Class java.security.KeyStore](https://cr.openjdk.org/~pminborg/panama/21/v1/javadoc/java.base/java/security/class-use/KeyStore.html?utm_source=chatgpt.com)

## Response:
7/12/2026, 9:29:35 PM

Berikut **cheat sheet Java Secure Communication** untuk **Java 17+**.

Fokusnya: **TLS/HTTPS, JSSE, HttpClient secure config, certificate validation, mTLS, keystore/truststore, hostname verification, secure socket, secure API communication, message-level security, replay protection, token handling, dan production checklist**.

# Java Secure Communication Cheat Sheet

---

# 1. Mental Model

```text id="cxla8d"
Secure Communication
├── Transport Security
│   ├── TLS
│   ├── HTTPS
│   ├── Certificate validation
│   ├── Hostname verification
│   └── mTLS
│
├── Application Security
│   ├── Authorization header
│   ├── API key
│   ├── OAuth2 token
│   ├── HMAC request signing
│   ├── Digital signature
│   └── Replay protection
│
├── Java APIs
│   ├── JSSE
│   ├── SSLContext
│   ├── SSLSocket
│   ├── SSLEngine
│   ├── KeyManager
│   ├── TrustManager
│   ├── KeyStore
│   └── java.net.http.HttpClient
│
└── Operations
    ├── certificate rotation
    ├── truststore management
    ├── secret management
    ├── observability
    └── incident response
```

Java Secure Socket Extension, atau **JSSE**, adalah framework Java untuk TLS. Oracle menjelaskan JSSE sebagai framework dan implementasi TLS yang menyediakan enkripsi data, server authentication, message integrity, dan optional client authentication untuk komunikasi aman di atas TCP/IP. ([Oracle Docs](https://docs.oracle.com/en/java/javase/21/security/java-secure-socket-extension-jsse-reference-guide.html?utm_source=chatgpt.com))

---

# 2. Secure Communication Layers

| Layer | Protects | Example |
|---|---|---|
| Network boundary | routing/firewall | security group, Kubernetes NetworkPolicy |
| Transport | confidentiality + integrity in transit | TLS/HTTPS |
| Endpoint identity | server/client authenticity | X.509 certificate |
| Application auth | who is calling | OAuth2, API key, mTLS identity |
| Message integrity | payload not tampered | HMAC, digital signature |
| Replay protection | old request cannot be reused | timestamp + nonce |
| Data-level crypto | payload protected beyond TLS | JWE/custom envelope encryption |
| Audit | traceability | request ID, token subject, certificate DN |

Rule utama:

```text id="mqlxma"
TLS protects the pipe.
Authentication identifies the caller.
Authorization decides what caller can do.
Message signing protects payload semantics.
Replay protection prevents reuse of captured valid messages.
```

---

# 3. Threat Model

Secure communication biasanya melindungi dari:

```text id="gxma3a"
eavesdropping
man-in-the-middle attack
server impersonation
client impersonation
payload tampering
credential theft
replay attack
downgrade attack
misdirected request
SSRF
leaked tokens in logs
expired/revoked certificate
wrong trust boundary
```

OWASP Transport Layer Security Cheat Sheet menyebut TLS dapat memberi beberapa manfaat security jika diimplementasikan dengan benar, terutama untuk melindungi koneksi client ke web application melalui HTTPS. ([OWASP Cheat Sheet Series](https://cheatsheetseries.owasp.org/cheatsheets/Transport_Layer_Security_Cheat_Sheet.html?utm_source=chatgpt.com))

---

# 4. Decision Matrix

| Need | Use |
|---|---|
| Secure HTTP call | HTTPS with `HttpClient` |
| Validate server identity | default certificate + hostname verification |
| Custom internal CA | custom truststore |
| Client authentication | mTLS with keystore |
| Raw secure TCP | `SSLSocket` |
| Non-blocking TLS engine | `SSLEngine` |
| Sign webhook/request | HMAC or digital signature |
| Protect against replay | timestamp + nonce + signature |
| Local-only process IPC | Unix domain socket / localhost + auth |
| Service-to-service identity | mTLS / workload identity / OAuth2 |
| Sensitive payload beyond TLS | message-level encryption/signature |
| Certificate/key storage | `KeyStore` / secret manager / KMS |

---

# 5. TLS Basics

TLS gives:

```text id="e675is"
confidentiality
integrity
server authentication
optional client authentication
```

TLS does **not** automatically give:

```text id="yhobsf"
business authorization
payload schema validation
replay protection at application layer
safe logging
safe token storage
SSRF protection
end-to-end encryption across intermediaries
```

NIST SP 800-52 Rev. 2 is a guideline for selecting, configuring, and using TLS implementations with NIST-recommended cryptographic algorithms. ([NIST Computer Security Resource Center](https://csrc.nist.gov/pubs/sp/800/52/r2/final?utm_source=chatgpt.com))

---

# 6. HTTPS with `HttpClient`

## 6.1 Basic Secure Client

```java id="lmst7h"
HttpClient client = HttpClient.newBuilder()
    .connectTimeout(Duration.ofSeconds(5))
    .version(HttpClient.Version.HTTP_2)
    .build();

HttpRequest request = HttpRequest.newBuilder()
    .uri(URI.create("https://api.example.com/orders"))
    .timeout(Duration.ofSeconds(10))
    .header("Accept", "application/json")
    .GET()
    .build();

HttpResponse<String> response = client.send(
    request,
    HttpResponse.BodyHandlers.ofString(StandardCharsets.UTF_8)
);
```

Default HTTPS memakai trust configuration JVM. Untuk kebanyakan public HTTPS endpoint, **jangan custom SSLContext** kecuali ada alasan jelas seperti private CA atau mTLS.

---

## 6.2 Secure Defaults Checklist for HTTP Client

```text id="zjv8cc"
[ ] URI uses https://
[ ] connect timeout set
[ ] request timeout set
[ ] TLS certificate validation enabled
[ ] hostname verification enabled
[ ] Authorization header not logged
[ ] redirects intentional
[ ] response status mapped
[ ] retry only if idempotent
[ ] response body size considered
```

---

# 7. Do Not Disable TLS Verification

Bad:

```text id="gy1ajt"
trust all certificates
disable hostname verification
ignore SSLHandshakeException
set jdk.internal.httpclient.disableHostnameVerification=true
```

Oracle networking documentation lists `jdk.internal.httpclient.disableHostnameVerification`; if set to true, hostname verification in SSL certificates is disabled. Treat this as a dangerous testing-only switch, not a production configuration. ([Oracle Docs](https://docs.oracle.com/en/java/javase/24/core/java-networking.html?embed=1&utm_source=chatgpt.com))

Rule keras:

```text id="4t2a8v"
Never disable certificate validation in production.
Never disable hostname verification in production.
Never use trust-all TrustManager in production.
```

---

# 8. SSLContext

`SSLContext` is the main object for TLS configuration.

```text id="v5ehv4"
SSLContext
├── KeyManager[]   -> your private key/certificate for client/server identity
├── TrustManager[] -> who you trust
└── SecureRandom   -> cryptographic randomness
```

---

# 9. Custom Truststore

Use case:

```text id="37czvo"
internal CA
self-hosted enterprise CA
on-prem service certificate
private service mesh CA
partner API with private trust anchor
```

## 9.1 Load Truststore

```java id="fmhc0e"
public static KeyStore loadKeyStore(
    Path path,
    char[] password
) throws GeneralSecurityException, IOException {
    KeyStore keyStore = KeyStore.getInstance("PKCS12");

    try (InputStream input = Files.newInputStream(path)) {
        keyStore.load(input, password);
    }

    return keyStore;
}
```

## 9.2 Build TrustManager

```java id="du2z1x"
public static TrustManager[] trustManagersFrom(
    KeyStore trustStore
) throws GeneralSecurityException {
    TrustManagerFactory factory = TrustManagerFactory.getInstance(
        TrustManagerFactory.getDefaultAlgorithm()
    );

    factory.init(trustStore);

    return factory.getTrustManagers();
}
```

## 9.3 Build SSLContext

```java id="pdg3zm"
public static SSLContext sslContextWithTrustStore(
    Path trustStorePath,
    char[] trustStorePassword
) throws GeneralSecurityException, IOException {
    KeyStore trustStore = loadKeyStore(trustStorePath, trustStorePassword);

    SSLContext sslContext = SSLContext.getInstance("TLS");

    sslContext.init(
        null,
        trustManagersFrom(trustStore),
        new SecureRandom()
    );

    return sslContext;
}
```

Use with `HttpClient`:

```java id="twe2kb"
SSLContext sslContext = sslContextWithTrustStore(
    Path.of("truststore.p12"),
    trustStorePassword
);

HttpClient client = HttpClient.newBuilder()
    .sslContext(sslContext)
    .connectTimeout(Duration.ofSeconds(5))
    .build();
```

---

# 10. mTLS Client

mTLS means both sides authenticate:

```text id="00c3xe"
Server proves identity with server certificate.
Client proves identity with client certificate.
```

Use case:

```text id="j4bptz"
service-to-service auth
partner integration
internal API
B2B gateway
legacy enterprise security
zero-trust-ish service identity
```

---

## 10.1 Load Client Keystore

Client keystore contains:

```text id="2j4fvz"
client private key
client certificate
certificate chain
```

```java id="6ajomc"
public static KeyManager[] keyManagersFrom(
    KeyStore keyStore,
    char[] keyPassword
) throws GeneralSecurityException {
    KeyManagerFactory factory = KeyManagerFactory.getInstance(
        KeyManagerFactory.getDefaultAlgorithm()
    );

    factory.init(keyStore, keyPassword);

    return factory.getKeyManagers();
}
```

---

## 10.2 SSLContext for mTLS

```java id="ao45kv"
public static SSLContext mtlsSslContext(
    Path keyStorePath,
    char[] keyStorePassword,
    char[] keyPassword,
    Path trustStorePath,
    char[] trustStorePassword
) throws GeneralSecurityException, IOException {
    KeyStore keyStore = loadKeyStore(keyStorePath, keyStorePassword);
    KeyStore trustStore = loadKeyStore(trustStorePath, trustStorePassword);

    SSLContext sslContext = SSLContext.getInstance("TLS");

    sslContext.init(
        keyManagersFrom(keyStore, keyPassword),
        trustManagersFrom(trustStore),
        new SecureRandom()
    );

    return sslContext;
}
```

Use:

```java id="y4kf1k"
SSLContext sslContext = mtlsSslContext(
    Path.of("client-keystore.p12"),
    keyStorePassword,
    keyPassword,
    Path.of("truststore.p12"),
    trustStorePassword
);

HttpClient client = HttpClient.newBuilder()
    .sslContext(sslContext)
    .connectTimeout(Duration.ofSeconds(5))
    .build();
```

---

# 11. Server-Side TLS / mTLS Concept

For server-side TLS, server needs:

```text id="61rrth"
private key
server certificate
certificate chain
```

For server-side mTLS, server also needs:

```text id="61jypj"
truststore containing trusted client CA/cert
client authentication enabled/required
authorization mapping from certificate identity to app principal
```

With many enterprise systems, TLS termination is handled by:

```text id="m7b1yr"
load balancer
API gateway
service mesh sidecar
reverse proxy
Kubernetes ingress
application server
```

Java app must still understand **which layer owns identity**.

---

# 12. Hostname Verification

Hostname verification checks that:

```text id="yzc92q"
requested hostname
matches
certificate identity / subject alternative name
```

Example:

```text id="4dnwqe"
URL: https://orders.internal.example.com
Certificate SAN must include orders.internal.example.com
```

Why it matters:

```text id="ig4q4v"
A valid certificate for a different host must not be accepted.
Otherwise MITM or misrouting becomes possible.
```

Rule:

```text id="tmuxw7"
Do not bypass hostname verification.
Fix the certificate SAN instead.
```

---

# 13. Certificate Validation

A certificate should be checked for:

```text id="7rr7co"
chain of trust
validity period
key usage / extended key usage
hostname
revocation policy if required
algorithm strength
trust anchor
```

Common failures:

| Failure | Meaning |
|---|---|
| expired certificate | cert validity ended |
| not yet valid | cert starts in future |
| unknown CA | truststore does not trust issuer |
| hostname mismatch | SAN does not match host |
| incomplete chain | server did not send full chain |
| weak algorithm | algorithm disabled by JVM/security policy |
| revoked certificate | revoked by CA, if revocation checked |

---

# 14. Truststore vs Keystore

| Store | Contains | Used For |
|---|---|---|
| Truststore | trusted CA/certs | verify peer |
| Keystore | own private key + certificate | prove own identity |
| Same physical file? | possible | but separation is cleaner |

Client calling HTTPS:

```text id="bzizvk"
truststore validates server
```

Client doing mTLS:

```text id="id6zv6"
keystore proves client identity
truststore validates server
```

Server doing mTLS:

```text id="boly5x"
keystore proves server identity
truststore validates client
```

---

# 15. KeyStore Types

| Type | Use |
|---|---|
| `PKCS12` | preferred portable keystore format |
| `JKS` | legacy Java keystore |
| `JCEKS` | legacy secret key storage |
| OS/cloud/HSM provider | production key protection where available |

Rule:

```text id="kt8kz1"
Prefer PKCS12 for Java keystore files.
Prefer secret manager/KMS/HSM for high-value production secrets.
```

---

# 16. Secure Raw TLS Socket

Use only if building custom protocol over TLS.

## 16.1 TLS Client Socket

```java id="8bh7sm"
SSLSocketFactory factory =
    (SSLSocketFactory) SSLSocketFactory.getDefault();

try (SSLSocket socket =
         (SSLSocket) factory.createSocket("example.com", 443)) {

    socket.setSoTimeout(10_000);
    socket.startHandshake();

    BufferedWriter writer = new BufferedWriter(
        new OutputStreamWriter(
            socket.getOutputStream(),
            StandardCharsets.US_ASCII
        )
    );

    writer.write("GET / HTTP/1.1\r\n");
    writer.write("Host: example.com\r\n");
    writer.write("Connection: close\r\n");
    writer.write("\r\n");
    writer.flush();

    try (BufferedReader reader = new BufferedReader(
        new InputStreamReader(
            socket.getInputStream(),
            StandardCharsets.UTF_8
        )
    )) {
        String line;

        while ((line = reader.readLine()) != null) {
            System.out.println(line);
        }
    }
}
```

Use `HttpClient` for HTTPS. Use `SSLSocket` only for custom TLS protocol.

---

## 16.2 TLS Server Socket

```java id="dr4k39"
SSLServerSocketFactory factory =
    (SSLServerSocketFactory) SSLServerSocketFactory.getDefault();

try (SSLServerSocket serverSocket =
         (SSLServerSocket) factory.createServerSocket(8443)) {

    while (true) {
        SSLSocket socket = (SSLSocket) serverSocket.accept();

        Thread.startVirtualThread(() -> handleSecureClient(socket));
    }
}
```

For real server TLS, configure keystore properly through JVM properties or custom `SSLContext`.

---

# 17. SSLEngine

`SSLEngine` is lower-level TLS engine for non-blocking I/O.

Use for:

```text id="429va2"
custom NIO server
Netty-like framework internals
non-blocking TLS over SocketChannel
custom protocol implementation
```

Avoid for normal backend application code.

```text id="ca9wci"
If you are writing business service code:
use HttpClient, framework, SSLSocket, or app server.
Do not manually implement SSLEngine unless building infrastructure.
```

---

# 18. Secure HTTP API Communication

Minimum baseline for service-to-service API:

```text id="j9l4sb"
HTTPS
timeout
authentication
authorization
request ID
safe logging
status handling
retry policy
rate limiting/bulkhead
secret masking
```

Example:

```java id="n7h7ko"
HttpRequest request = HttpRequest.newBuilder()
    .uri(URI.create("https://api.partner.example.com/orders"))
    .timeout(Duration.ofSeconds(10))
    .header("Accept", "application/json")
    .header("Content-Type", "application/json")
    .header("Authorization", "Bearer " + accessToken)
    .header("X-Request-Id", requestId)
    .POST(HttpRequest.BodyPublishers.ofString(json, StandardCharsets.UTF_8))
    .build();
```

---

# 19. Token Handling

## 19.1 Bearer Token

```java id="b860n5"
HttpRequest request = HttpRequest.newBuilder()
    .uri(uri)
    .header("Authorization", "Bearer " + accessToken)
    .GET()
    .build();
```

Rules:

```text id="w1cubz"
Do not put token in URL.
Do not log Authorization header.
Use short-lived access tokens.
Refresh securely.
Validate issuer/audience/expiry on server side.
```

---

## 19.2 API Key

```java id="93hvrr"
HttpRequest request = HttpRequest.newBuilder()
    .uri(uri)
    .header("X-API-Key", apiKey)
    .GET()
    .build();
```

Rules:

```text id="s3xkwb"
Prefer header over query parameter.
Rotate API keys.
Scope API keys.
Mask in logs.
Do not hardcode in source.
```

---

# 20. HMAC Request Signing

Use HMAC when both parties share a secret and you need message-level integrity/authentication.

## 20.1 Canonical Request

```text id="bqvp0p"
METHOD
PATH
QUERY
TIMESTAMP
NONCE
SHA256_BODY_HASH
```

Example canonical string:

```java id="sw4vcb"
public static String canonicalRequest(
    String method,
    URI uri,
    String timestamp,
    String nonce,
    byte[] body
) throws GeneralSecurityException {
    String bodyHash = HexFormat.of().formatHex(
        MessageDigest.getInstance("SHA-256").digest(body)
    );

    return String.join(
        "\n",
        method.toUpperCase(Locale.ROOT),
        uri.getPath(),
        uri.getRawQuery() == null ? "" : uri.getRawQuery(),
        timestamp,
        nonce,
        bodyHash
    );
}
```

---

## 20.2 HMAC-SHA256

```java id="2rf1dk"
public static String hmacSha256Base64(
    byte[] secret,
    String message
) throws GeneralSecurityException {
    Mac mac = Mac.getInstance("HmacSHA256");

    mac.init(new SecretKeySpec(secret, "HmacSHA256"));

    byte[] tag = mac.doFinal(message.getBytes(StandardCharsets.UTF_8));

    return Base64.getEncoder().encodeToString(tag);
}
```

---

## 20.3 Send Signed Request

```java id="x0ikdo"
String timestamp = Instant.now().toString();
String nonce = UUID.randomUUID().toString();

byte[] body = json.getBytes(StandardCharsets.UTF_8);

String canonical = canonicalRequest(
    "POST",
    uri,
    timestamp,
    nonce,
    body
);

String signature = hmacSha256Base64(secret, canonical);

HttpRequest request = HttpRequest.newBuilder()
    .uri(uri)
    .timeout(Duration.ofSeconds(10))
    .header("Content-Type", "application/json")
    .header("X-Timestamp", timestamp)
    .header("X-Nonce", nonce)
    .header("X-Signature", signature)
    .POST(HttpRequest.BodyPublishers.ofByteArray(body))
    .build();
```

---

# 21. Replay Protection

HMAC alone does not stop replay if attacker captures valid request.

Add:

```text id="ixgsnh"
timestamp
nonce
short validity window
nonce cache
request body hash
canonical path/query
```

Server-side validation:

```text id="3d2vsl"
1. Parse timestamp.
2. Reject if outside allowed clock skew, e.g. 5 minutes.
3. Reject if nonce already used.
4. Recompute signature from canonical request.
5. Constant-time compare signature.
6. Process request.
7. Store nonce until window expires.
```

Constant-time compare:

```java id="z7lu0o"
boolean valid = MessageDigest.isEqual(
    expectedSignatureBytes,
    actualSignatureBytes
);
```

---

# 22. Digital Signature Communication

Use digital signature when:

```text id="9gzs2e"
sender signs with private key
receiver verifies with public key
receiver must not know signing secret
many receivers verify same signed message
auditability matters
```

Example use cases:

```text id="frve35"
signed webhook
signed document
software update metadata
B2B message exchange
non-repudiation-style audit
```

For implementation, use `Signature`, for example `Ed25519` where ecosystem supports it, or RSA-PSS/ECDSA according to interoperability requirements.

---

# 23. Message-Level Encryption

TLS protects data in transit between endpoints. Message-level encryption protects payload beyond transport.

Use when:

```text id="ok7f1a"
payload passes through intermediaries
message stored in queue/database
end-to-end confidentiality required
partner gateway terminates TLS
sensitive payload must stay encrypted at rest
```

Pattern:

```text id="6fa3v2"
1. Generate data encryption key.
2. Encrypt payload with AES-GCM.
3. Encrypt/wrap data key with recipient public key or KMS.
4. Send encrypted payload + encrypted key + nonce + keyId.
```

Do not confuse:

| Protection | Covers |
|---|---|
| TLS | connection |
| HMAC | message integrity/authentication |
| Digital signature | public verifiable authenticity |
| AES-GCM payload encryption | payload confidentiality + integrity |
| OAuth2 token | caller authorization context |

---

# 24. Certificate Pinning

Certificate pinning means client accepts only specific certificate or public key fingerprint.

Use rarely:

```text id="ba42o1"
mobile app high-risk endpoint
closed B2B integration
highly controlled environment
```

Risks:

```text id="1141si"
certificate rotation outage
emergency CA change difficult
operational complexity
pin mismatch incident
```

Prefer:

```text id="cau2dw"
private CA truststore
mTLS
short-lived certificates
service mesh identity
proper certificate monitoring
```

---

# 25. Secure Redirect Handling

```java id="0s3sj4"
HttpClient client = HttpClient.newBuilder()
    .followRedirects(HttpClient.Redirect.NORMAL)
    .build();
```

Security rules:

```text id="zw8t77"
Do not forward Authorization header to untrusted redirected host.
Prefer no redirect for API clients unless required.
Validate final host if redirect is allowed.
Avoid HTTP downgrade.
```

For highly sensitive APIs:

```java id="kapucn"
HttpClient client = HttpClient.newBuilder()
    .followRedirects(HttpClient.Redirect.NEVER)
    .build();
```

---

# 26. SSRF Protection for Outbound Calls

If user controls URL, protect outbound fetch.

Bad:

```java id="xbpy6p"
URI uri = URI.create(userInputUrl);
client.send(HttpRequest.newBuilder(uri).GET().build(), BodyHandlers.ofString());
```

Better allowlist:

```java id="yh10w7"
public static URI validateAllowedHttpsUri(String raw) {
    URI uri = URI.create(raw);

    if (!"https".equalsIgnoreCase(uri.getScheme())) {
        throw new IllegalArgumentException("Only HTTPS is allowed");
    }

    String host = uri.getHost();

    if (host == null || !host.endsWith(".trusted.example.com")) {
        throw new IllegalArgumentException("Host not allowed");
    }

    return uri;
}
```

SSRF-sensitive targets:

```text id="5pnah9"
localhost
127.0.0.1
::1
0.0.0.0
169.254.169.254
private IP ranges
internal DNS
file://
jar://
ftp://
gopher://
```

Rule:

```text id="znhhrz"
Use allowlist, not only blocklist.
```

---

# 27. HSTS

For web applications served to browsers, HSTS tells browsers to use HTTPS.

Header:

```text id="2gq4nh"
Strict-Transport-Security: max-age=31536000; includeSubDomains
```

With preload:

```text id="3hrtw1"
Strict-Transport-Security: max-age=31536000; includeSubDomains; preload
```

OWASP TLS guidance covers implementing transport layer protection for applications with TLS, particularly HTTPS; HSTS is commonly part of browser-facing HTTPS hardening. ([OWASP Cheat Sheet Series](https://cheatsheetseries.owasp.org/cheatsheets/Transport_Layer_Security_Cheat_Sheet.html?utm_source=chatgpt.com))

Backend Java app may set this header directly or via gateway/reverse proxy.

---

# 28. Secure Logging

Log:

```text id="8lngtf"
method
host
path template
status
duration
requestId
peer certificate subject if needed
token subject, not token
mTLS client DN if needed
error category
```

Do not log:

```text id="e7yk03"
Authorization
Cookie
Set-Cookie
API key
client secret
private key
raw certificate private key
access token
refresh token
full query string if it contains secrets
request body with PII/secrets
```

Mask helper:

```java id="f8wljs"
public static String maskSecret(String value) {
    if (value == null || value.length() <= 6) {
        return "****";
    }

    return value.substring(0, 3)
        + "****"
        + value.substring(value.length() - 3);
}
```

---

# 29. Secure Configuration

Prefer:

```text id="bgf46h"
environment variable for non-secret config
secret manager for secrets
KMS for master keys
keystore/truststore mounted with restricted permissions
short-lived credentials
rotation automation
```

Avoid:

```text id="l2vzmg"
hardcoded secrets
committed keystore passwords
printing JVM properties
logging environment variables
embedding private keys in code
```

Example:

```java id="t4eomk"
String truststorePath = System.getenv("TRUSTSTORE_PATH");
String truststorePassword = System.getenv("TRUSTSTORE_PASSWORD");
```

Better for production:

```text id="a3c8hz"
retrieve truststore password from secret manager
mount keystore as read-only secret
restrict filesystem permissions
```

---

# 30. Kubernetes / Cloud Notes

In Kubernetes/cloud deployments:

```text id="ay1gws"
TLS may terminate at ingress/load balancer
mTLS may be handled by service mesh
pod-to-pod traffic may still require encryption depending policy
secrets are mounted as files/env vars
certificate rotation may be automated
trust bundle may be injected
```

Questions to ask:

```text id="3iejt0"
Where does TLS terminate?
Who validates client identity?
Does app receive original TLS client cert?
Are internal calls encrypted?
How are certificates rotated?
Who owns truststore updates?
Are secrets mounted read-only?
```

---

# 31. Common Java Secure Communication Patterns

## 31.1 HTTPS + Bearer Token

```text id="bp6q8x"
HTTPS protects transport.
Bearer token identifies caller.
Server validates token.
Server authorizes action.
```

Good for:

```text id="lf729y"
OAuth2 resource server
service-to-service via identity provider
API gateway backed auth
```

---

## 31.2 HTTPS + API Key

```text id="qvlpur"
HTTPS protects transport.
API key identifies integration/application.
Server checks key, scope, quota.
```

Good for:

```text id="0zc6z6"
simple partner integration
internal tools
legacy service access
```

Weakness:

```text id="a0q061"
API key is bearer credential.
If leaked, attacker can use it.
Needs rotation and scope.
```

---

## 31.3 HTTPS + HMAC Signature

```text id="rd70bz"
HTTPS protects transport.
HMAC authenticates message.
Timestamp/nonce prevents replay.
```

Good for:

```text id="2hiijt"
webhooks
B2B callback
high-integrity request signing
```

---

## 31.4 mTLS

```text id="5nvgbq"
TLS validates server.
Client certificate validates client.
App maps cert identity to principal/scope.
```

Good for:

```text id="w9rk4r"
service-to-service
enterprise internal API
partner private connectivity
service mesh
```

Important:

```text id="1g4m55"
mTLS authenticates client identity at transport layer.
Application still needs authorization.
```

---

## 31.5 Message Encryption

```text id="dl1e8g"
Payload encrypted before sending.
TLS still used.
Intermediaries cannot read payload.
```

Good for:

```text id="afuvfy"
sensitive document exchange
queue-based communication
multi-hop partner integration
```

---

# 32. Raw Secure Socket Framing

TLS secures bytes, not application messages. You still need framing.

Bad assumption:

```text id="0e5r64"
one write == one read
```

Use:

```text id="v3ky80"
newline-delimited frame
length-prefixed frame
fixed frame
protocol parser
```

Length-prefixed secure socket:

```java id="mzpixl"
DataOutputStream out = new DataOutputStream(socket.getOutputStream());

byte[] payload = json.getBytes(StandardCharsets.UTF_8);

out.writeInt(payload.length);
out.write(payload);
out.flush();
```

Reader:

```java id="pzpsqn"
DataInputStream in = new DataInputStream(socket.getInputStream());

int length = in.readInt();

if (length < 0 || length > 10_000_000) {
    throw new IOException("Invalid frame length");
}

byte[] payload = in.readNBytes(length);
```

Rule:

```text id="a4hs4y"
TLS does not remove need for protocol framing and size limits.
```

---

# 33. Error Handling

Do not expose internal TLS/security details to external caller.

Bad:

```java id="cwstcb"
return "SSLHandshakeException: unable to find valid certification path";
```

Better external:

```json id="0fn9sm"
{
  "code": "UPSTREAM_UNAVAILABLE",
  "message": "Unable to connect to upstream service"
}
```

Internal log:

```text id="39qv5d"
errorType=SSLHandshakeException
host=api.partner.example.com
requestId=...
```

Rule:

```text id="vm1k5z"
Log enough for operators.
Expose minimal safe error to clients.
Never include secrets.
```

---

# 34. Common Exceptions

| Exception | Common Meaning |
|---|---|
| `SSLHandshakeException` | cert/trust/protocol/cipher issue |
| `SSLPeerUnverifiedException` | peer identity not verified |
| `CertificateException` | invalid certificate |
| `UnknownHostException` | DNS failure |
| `ConnectException` | refused/unreachable |
| `SocketTimeoutException` | read/connect timeout |
| `HttpTimeoutException` | HTTP request timeout |
| `AccessDeniedException` | file permission for keystore/truststore |
| `UnrecoverableKeyException` | wrong key password |
| `KeyStoreException` | keystore access/config error |

Pattern:

```java id="lgen6y"
try {
    return client.send(request, HttpResponse.BodyHandlers.ofString());
} catch (SSLHandshakeException e) {
    throw new SecureChannelException("TLS handshake failed", e);
} catch (HttpTimeoutException e) {
    throw new DownstreamTimeoutException("HTTP timeout", e);
} catch (IOException e) {
    throw new DownstreamIOException("HTTP I/O error", e);
} catch (InterruptedException e) {
    Thread.currentThread().interrupt();
    throw new RuntimeException("Interrupted during HTTP call", e);
}
```

---

# 35. Certificate Rotation

Rotation checklist:

```text id="dpivj0"
[ ] Know certificate expiry date.
[ ] Monitor expiry.
[ ] Automate renewal if possible.
[ ] Deploy new cert before old expires.
[ ] Ensure full chain is served.
[ ] Ensure SAN contains correct hostnames.
[ ] Update truststore before switching private CA.
[ ] Test mTLS client cert rollover.
[ ] Support overlapping old/new trust during migration.
```

For mTLS:

```text id="qfv6w3"
server should trust old and new client CA/cert during transition
client should trust old and new server CA during transition
app authorization mapping should handle new certificate subject/SPIFFE ID/etc.
```

---

# 36. Secure Communication in On-Prem / Cloud

## On-Prem

```text id="2k52dc"
private CA common
custom truststore common
proxy common
legacy TLS common
certificate chain issues common
firewall/proxy inspection possible
```

## AWS/Azure

```text id="z3b9e7"
managed certificates
load balancer TLS termination
private CA options
secret manager / key vault
KMS integration
managed identity / workload identity
```

## Kubernetes

```text id="k862cz"
ingress TLS
service mesh mTLS
mounted trust bundle
rotating secrets
pod identity
network policies
```

Senior rule:

```text id="y1voil"
Always identify where identity is proven:
client app?
sidecar?
gateway?
load balancer?
application server?
```

---

# 37. Anti-Patterns

| Anti-pattern | Risk |
|---|---|
| HTTP for sensitive traffic | eavesdropping/tampering |
| Trust-all certificate manager | MITM |
| Disable hostname verification | MITM |
| Put token in URL | logs/browser/proxy leak |
| Log Authorization/Cookie | credential leak |
| Long-lived static API key | large blast radius |
| No timeout | hanging resources |
| Retry unsafe POST | duplicate side effects |
| HMAC without timestamp/nonce | replay attack |
| mTLS but no app authorization | authenticated but overprivileged |
| Certificate pinning without rotation plan | outage |
| User-controlled outbound URL | SSRF |
| Wildcard CORS with credentials | browser security issue |
| Full request/response body logging | PII/secret leak |
| Blindly trusting `X-Forwarded-*` | spoofing unless trusted proxy |
| Assuming TLS termination means app sees client cert | often false |

---

# 38. Production Checklist

```text id="fs270q"
Transport
[ ] HTTPS/TLS used for sensitive traffic?
[ ] TLS certificate validation enabled?
[ ] Hostname verification enabled?
[ ] TLS versions/ciphers comply with org policy?
[ ] Redirect policy safe?

Trust
[ ] Truststore source known?
[ ] Private/internal CA configured?
[ ] Certificate chain complete?
[ ] Certificate expiry monitored?
[ ] Rotation plan tested?

Identity
[ ] Server identity validated?
[ ] Client identity needed?
[ ] mTLS configured if needed?
[ ] Certificate identity mapped to app principal?
[ ] Authorization enforced after authentication?

Application Security
[ ] Token/API key in header, not URL?
[ ] HMAC/signature used where message integrity needed?
[ ] Replay protection implemented?
[ ] Request body size bounded?
[ ] User-controlled URLs allowlisted?

Reliability
[ ] Connect timeout set?
[ ] Request/read timeout set?
[ ] Retry bounded and idempotent?
[ ] Bulkhead/rate limit exists?
[ ] SSLHandshakeException monitored?

Operations
[ ] Secrets not in source?
[ ] Secrets not logged?
[ ] Keystore/truststore permissions restricted?
[ ] Rotation documented?
[ ] Observability includes requestId/status/duration/error type?
```

---

# 39. Debugging Checklist

When HTTPS fails:

```text id="x4fw4u"
[ ] Is URL hostname exactly in certificate SAN?
[ ] Is certificate expired?
[ ] Is certificate chain complete?
[ ] Is issuing CA trusted by JVM truststore?
[ ] Is custom truststore loaded?
[ ] Is key password correct?
[ ] Is keystore type correct?
[ ] Is server requiring client certificate?
[ ] Is client sending correct cert?
[ ] Is proxy intercepting TLS?
[ ] Is TLS version/cipher blocked?
[ ] Is system clock correct?
```

Helpful JVM debug flag for local diagnosis:

```bash id="c705pv"
-Djavax.net.debug=ssl,handshake
```

Warning:

```text id="xh3a14"
TLS debug logs can contain sensitive operational details.
Do not enable broadly in production.
```

---

# 40. Decision Guide

```text id="spdzsa"
Calling public HTTPS API?
-> default HttpClient + timeout + token handling

Calling internal private CA service?
-> custom truststore + default hostname verification

Need client identity at transport layer?
-> mTLS with keystore + truststore

Need payload integrity beyond TLS?
-> HMAC signature or digital signature

Need replay protection?
-> timestamp + nonce + signature + nonce cache

Need sensitive payload hidden from gateway/queue?
-> message-level AES-GCM/envelope encryption

Building custom secure protocol?
-> SSLSocket or framework; define framing and limits

Building NIO TLS framework?
-> SSLEngine, but avoid unless infrastructure work

User can provide target URL?
-> strict allowlist + SSRF protections

Need local IPC only?
-> Unix domain socket or localhost binding + auth
```

---

# 41. Compact Summary

```text id="flx5x6"
TLS / HTTPS
- protects communication channel
- provides confidentiality, integrity, server authentication
- optional client authentication via mTLS

JSSE
- Java TLS framework
- SSLContext, KeyManager, TrustManager, SSLSocket, SSLEngine

HttpClient
- default choice for HTTPS call
- set connect timeout and request timeout
- keep certificate and hostname verification enabled

Truststore
- who you trust
- validates peer certificate

Keystore
- who you are
- contains private key and certificate

mTLS
- both client and server authenticate with certificates
- still needs application authorization

HMAC Signing
- protects message integrity with shared secret
- add timestamp and nonce to prevent replay

Digital Signature
- sender signs with private key
- receiver verifies with public key

Secure Logging
- log method/host/path/status/duration/requestId
- never log tokens, cookies, secrets, private keys

Production Rules
- no trust-all
- no hostname verification bypass
- no tokens in URL
- timeout everything
- rotate certificates/secrets
- allowlist user-controlled outbound URLs
```

Kesimpulan praktis: **secure communication di Java bukan hanya “pakai HTTPS”.** Yang harus benar adalah **truststore, hostname verification, certificate lifecycle, mTLS identity mapping, token handling, replay protection, timeout, retry discipline, SSRF defense, dan secret-safe logging**.

---

**Sources:**

- [Java Secure Socket Extension (JSSE) Reference Guide](https://docs.oracle.com/en/java/javase/21/security/java-secure-socket-extension-jsse-reference-guide.html?utm_source=chatgpt.com)
- [Transport Layer Security Cheat Sheet](https://cheatsheetseries.owasp.org/cheatsheets/Transport_Layer_Security_Cheat_Sheet.html?utm_source=chatgpt.com)
- [SP 800-52 Rev. 2, Guidelines for the Selection, Configuration ...](https://csrc.nist.gov/pubs/sp/800/52/r2/final?utm_source=chatgpt.com)

## Response:
7/12/2026, 9:39:32 PM

Berikut **cheat sheet Java Public Key Infrastructure / PKI** untuk **Java 17+**.

Fokusnya: **X.509 certificate, CA, trust chain, keystore, truststore, keytool, CSR, certificate validation, CertPath, mTLS, revocation, hostname/SAN, dan operational PKI**.

# Java Public Key Infrastructure Cheat Sheet

---

# 1. Mental Model

```text
PKI
├── Identity
│   ├── subject
│   ├── DNS name / SAN
│   ├── organization
│   └── service identity
│
├── Key Pair
│   ├── private key  -> secret, proves identity / signs
│   └── public key   -> distributed inside certificate
│
├── Certificate
│   ├── X.509 certificate
│   ├── public key
│   ├── subject
│   ├── issuer
│   ├── validity period
│   ├── extensions
│   └── signature from issuer CA
│
├── Certificate Authority
│   ├── root CA
│   ├── intermediate CA
│   └── issuing CA
│
├── Trust
│   ├── trust anchor
│   ├── certificate chain
│   ├── truststore
│   └── certificate path validation
│
└── Java APIs / Tools
    ├── keytool
    ├── KeyStore
    ├── CertificateFactory
    ├── CertPathValidator
    ├── TrustManager
    ├── KeyManager
    ├── SSLContext
    └── HttpClient / SSLSocket
```

RFC 5280 adalah profil Internet untuk **X.509 v3 certificate** dan **X.509 v2 certificate revocation list / CRL**, jadi banyak istilah PKI modern seperti certificate, chain, extensions, dan revocation berakar pada model X.509 ini. ([IETF Datatracker](https://datatracker.ietf.org/doc/html/rfc5280?utm_source=chatgpt.com))

---

# 2. PKI Core Terms

| Term | Arti Praktis |
|---|---|
| Private key | Rahasia; jangan dibagikan |
| Public key | Boleh dibagikan; ada di certificate |
| Certificate | Public key + identity + metadata + signature CA |
| Subject | Pemilik certificate |
| Issuer | CA yang menerbitkan certificate |
| Root CA | Trust anchor tertinggi |
| Intermediate CA | CA di tengah chain |
| Leaf / end-entity cert | Certificate untuk server/client/user |
| CSR | Certificate Signing Request |
| Truststore | Daftar CA/cert yang dipercaya |
| Keystore | Private key + certificate milik aplikasi |
| Certificate chain | Leaf → intermediate → root |
| SAN | Subject Alternative Name; identitas host modern |
| CRL | Certificate Revocation List |
| OCSP | Online Certificate Status Protocol |
| mTLS | Mutual TLS; client dan server sama-sama pakai cert |

---

# 3. PKI Decision Matrix

| Need | PKI Object / Java API |
|---|---|
| Aplikasi membuktikan identitasnya | keystore |
| Aplikasi mempercayai server/peer | truststore |
| Generate key pair | `keytool -genkeypair` / `KeyPairGenerator` |
| Request certificate ke CA | CSR |
| Import signed certificate | `keytool -importcert` |
| Import trusted CA | truststore |
| Validate certificate chain | `CertPathValidator` |
| HTTPS client custom trust | `SSLContext` + `TrustManager` |
| mTLS client | `SSLContext` + `KeyManager` + `TrustManager` |
| Inspect certificate | `keytool -list -v` / `X509Certificate` |
| Check hostname | TLS hostname verification |
| Check revocation | CRL / OCSP config |
| Rotate certificate | new key/cert + overlapping trust |

---

# 4. Certificate Chain

Typical server certificate chain:

```text
Root CA
  ↓ signs
Intermediate CA
  ↓ signs
Server Certificate
```

During TLS validation, client checks roughly:

```text
1. Server sends leaf certificate and usually intermediate chain.
2. Client builds path from leaf to trusted root/intermediate.
3. Client verifies signatures.
4. Client checks validity period.
5. Client checks certificate usage constraints.
6. Client checks hostname against SAN.
7. Client may check revocation.
```

RFC 5280 profiles the certificate and CRL structures used for this Internet X.509 PKI model; Java’s certificate path APIs are the Java-side tools for certificate path building and validation. ([IETF Datatracker](https://datatracker.ietf.org/doc/html/rfc5280?utm_source=chatgpt.com))

---

# 5. Truststore vs Keystore

| Store | Contains | Used By | Purpose |
|---|---|---|---|
| Truststore | trusted CA/cert public material | `TrustManager` | verify peer |
| Keystore | private key + certificate chain | `KeyManager` | prove own identity |

Example:

```text
HTTPS client without mTLS:
- needs truststore
- does not need client keystore

HTTPS client with mTLS:
- needs truststore to trust server
- needs keystore to present client certificate

HTTPS server:
- needs keystore to present server certificate

HTTPS server with mTLS:
- needs keystore to present server certificate
- needs truststore to trust client certificate
```

In JSSE, `X509KeyManager` manages certificate-based key pairs used to authenticate the local side, while `X509TrustManager` decides which X.509 certificates can authenticate the remote side. ([Oracle Docs](https://docs.oracle.com/en/java/javase/21/docs/api/java.base/javax/net/ssl/package-summary.html?utm_source=chatgpt.com))

---

# 6. Common File Formats

| Format | Meaning |
|---|---|
| `.pem` | Base64 text with `-----BEGIN ...-----` |
| `.crt` / `.cer` | certificate, PEM or DER |
| `.der` | binary ASN.1 DER |
| `.p12` / `.pfx` | PKCS#12 keystore, may contain private key + cert |
| `.jks` | legacy Java KeyStore |
| `.csr` | certificate signing request |
| `.key` | private key |
| `.p7b` / `.p7c` | PKCS#7 certificate chain, usually no private key |

Java `keytool` can import X.509 certificates and PKCS#7 certificate chains; imported data may be binary or printable Base64/PEM-style encoding. ([Oracle Docs](https://docs.oracle.com/en/java/javase/21/docs/specs/man/keytool.html?utm_source=chatgpt.com))

---

# 7. Java PKI Main APIs

| API | Purpose |
|---|---|
| `KeyStore` | load/save keys and certificates |
| `X509Certificate` | inspect X.509 cert |
| `CertificateFactory` | parse certificate/chain |
| `CertPath` | certificate path |
| `CertPathValidator` | validate cert chain |
| `PKIXParameters` | validation trust anchors / rules |
| `TrustManagerFactory` | build TLS trust managers |
| `KeyManagerFactory` | build TLS key managers |
| `SSLContext` | TLS context |
| `HttpsURLConnection` | legacy HTTPS |
| `HttpClient` | modern HTTPS client |
| `SSLSocket` | raw TLS socket |

Java Security Standard Algorithm Names documents standard names for algorithms, certificate types, and keystore types used by Java security APIs. ([Oracle Docs](https://docs.oracle.com/en/java/javase/24/docs/specs/security/standard-names.html?utm_source=chatgpt.com))

---

# 8. `keytool` Basics

`keytool` is Java’s built-in key and certificate management utility. Oracle describes it as a tool to administer public/private key pairs, associated certificates, and cached public keys of communicating peers. ([cr.openjdk.org](https://cr.openjdk.org/~jjg/8261930/docs/specs/man/keytool.html?utm_source=chatgpt.com))

## 8.1 List Keystore

```bash
keytool -list \
  -v \
  -keystore app-keystore.p12 \
  -storetype PKCS12
```

## 8.2 List Truststore

```bash
keytool -list \
  -v \
  -keystore app-truststore.p12 \
  -storetype PKCS12
```

## 8.3 Show One Alias

```bash
keytool -list \
  -v \
  -alias app-server \
  -keystore app-keystore.p12 \
  -storetype PKCS12
```

---

# 9. Generate Key Pair

## 9.1 Generate Server Key Pair

```bash
keytool -genkeypair \
  -alias app-server \
  -keyalg RSA \
  -keysize 3072 \
  -sigalg SHA384withRSA \
  -validity 365 \
  -keystore app-keystore.p12 \
  -storetype PKCS12 \
  -dname "CN=app.internal.example.com,O=Example,L=Jakarta,C=ID" \
  -ext "SAN=dns:app.internal.example.com,dns:app"
```

Important:

```text
CN alone is not enough for modern TLS hostname identity.
Use SAN for DNS names.
```

## 9.2 Generate EC Key Pair

```bash
keytool -genkeypair \
  -alias app-server-ec \
  -keyalg EC \
  -groupname secp256r1 \
  -sigalg SHA256withECDSA \
  -validity 365 \
  -keystore app-keystore.p12 \
  -storetype PKCS12 \
  -dname "CN=app.internal.example.com,O=Example,C=ID" \
  -ext "SAN=dns:app.internal.example.com"
```

---

# 10. CSR Workflow

CSR means: “CA, please sign a certificate for this public key and identity.”

## 10.1 Generate CSR

```bash
keytool -certreq \
  -alias app-server \
  -file app-server.csr \
  -keystore app-keystore.p12 \
  -storetype PKCS12 \
  -ext "SAN=dns:app.internal.example.com,dns:app"
```

Send CSR to CA.

CA returns usually:

```text
server certificate
intermediate certificate(s)
root certificate, sometimes separately
```

---

## 10.2 Import CA Root / Intermediate into Truststore

```bash
keytool -importcert \
  -alias example-root-ca \
  -file root-ca.crt \
  -keystore app-truststore.p12 \
  -storetype PKCS12 \
  -trustcacerts
```

Intermediate:

```bash
keytool -importcert \
  -alias example-intermediate-ca \
  -file intermediate-ca.crt \
  -keystore app-truststore.p12 \
  -storetype PKCS12 \
  -trustcacerts
```

---

## 10.3 Import Signed Certificate Chain into Keystore

Usually import CA certs first into keystore if needed:

```bash
keytool -importcert \
  -alias example-root-ca \
  -file root-ca.crt \
  -keystore app-keystore.p12 \
  -storetype PKCS12 \
  -trustcacerts
```

```bash
keytool -importcert \
  -alias example-intermediate-ca \
  -file intermediate-ca.crt \
  -keystore app-keystore.p12 \
  -storetype PKCS12 \
  -trustcacerts
```

Then import signed server certificate under the **same alias** as the private key:

```bash
keytool -importcert \
  -alias app-server \
  -file app-server.crt \
  -keystore app-keystore.p12 \
  -storetype PKCS12
```

Rule:

```text
Signed leaf certificate must be imported to the alias that owns the private key.
Trusted CA certs use separate aliases.
```

---

# 11. Export Certificate

```bash
keytool -exportcert \
  -alias app-server \
  -rfc \
  -file app-server-public.crt \
  -keystore app-keystore.p12 \
  -storetype PKCS12
```

`-rfc` outputs PEM-style text.

---

# 12. Keystore Type Selection

| Type | Use |
|---|---|
| `PKCS12` | preferred portable keystore |
| `JKS` | legacy Java keystore |
| `JCEKS` | older secret-key capable keystore |
| cloud/HSM/provider | stronger production key protection |

Rule:

```text
Prefer PKCS12 for Java file-based keystore/truststore.
Use cloud KMS/HSM/secret manager for higher-value production keys.
Avoid relying on global JVM cacerts for application-specific trust.
```

---

# 13. Loading KeyStore in Java

```java
public static KeyStore loadKeyStore(
    Path path,
    char[] password
) throws GeneralSecurityException, IOException {
    KeyStore keyStore = KeyStore.getInstance("PKCS12");

    try (InputStream input = Files.newInputStream(path)) {
        keyStore.load(input, password);
    }

    return keyStore;
}
```

Usage:

```java
KeyStore trustStore = loadKeyStore(
    Path.of("app-truststore.p12"),
    trustStorePassword
);
```

---

# 14. Build TrustManager

```java
public static TrustManager[] trustManagersFrom(
    KeyStore trustStore
) throws GeneralSecurityException {
    TrustManagerFactory factory = TrustManagerFactory.getInstance(
        TrustManagerFactory.getDefaultAlgorithm()
    );

    factory.init(trustStore);

    return factory.getTrustManagers();
}
```

Use case:

```text
custom truststore
private CA
on-prem CA
partner CA
test CA
```

---

# 15. Build KeyManager

```java
public static KeyManager[] keyManagersFrom(
    KeyStore keyStore,
    char[] keyPassword
) throws GeneralSecurityException {
    KeyManagerFactory factory = KeyManagerFactory.getInstance(
        KeyManagerFactory.getDefaultAlgorithm()
    );

    factory.init(keyStore, keyPassword);

    return factory.getKeyManagers();
}
```

Use case:

```text
mTLS client certificate
HTTPS server certificate
raw TLS socket identity
```

---

# 16. Build SSLContext

## 16.1 Truststore Only

```java
public static SSLContext sslContextWithTrustStore(
    KeyStore trustStore
) throws GeneralSecurityException {
    SSLContext sslContext = SSLContext.getInstance("TLS");

    sslContext.init(
        null,
        trustManagersFrom(trustStore),
        new SecureRandom()
    );

    return sslContext;
}
```

## 16.2 mTLS

```java
public static SSLContext mtlsSslContext(
    KeyStore keyStore,
    char[] keyPassword,
    KeyStore trustStore
) throws GeneralSecurityException {
    SSLContext sslContext = SSLContext.getInstance("TLS");

    sslContext.init(
        keyManagersFrom(keyStore, keyPassword),
        trustManagersFrom(trustStore),
        new SecureRandom()
    );

    return sslContext;
}
```

---

# 17. Use PKI with Java HttpClient

## 17.1 Custom Truststore

```java
KeyStore trustStore = loadKeyStore(
    Path.of("app-truststore.p12"),
    trustStorePassword
);

SSLContext sslContext = sslContextWithTrustStore(trustStore);

HttpClient client = HttpClient.newBuilder()
    .sslContext(sslContext)
    .connectTimeout(Duration.ofSeconds(5))
    .build();
```

## 17.2 mTLS Client

```java
KeyStore keyStore = loadKeyStore(
    Path.of("client-keystore.p12"),
    keyStorePassword
);

KeyStore trustStore = loadKeyStore(
    Path.of("app-truststore.p12"),
    trustStorePassword
);

SSLContext sslContext = mtlsSslContext(
    keyStore,
    keyPassword,
    trustStore
);

HttpClient client = HttpClient.newBuilder()
    .sslContext(sslContext)
    .connectTimeout(Duration.ofSeconds(5))
    .build();
```

Request:

```java
HttpRequest request = HttpRequest.newBuilder()
    .uri(URI.create("https://orders.internal.example.com/api/orders"))
    .timeout(Duration.ofSeconds(10))
    .GET()
    .build();

HttpResponse<String> response = client.send(
    request,
    HttpResponse.BodyHandlers.ofString(StandardCharsets.UTF_8)
);
```

---

# 18. Parse X.509 Certificate

```java
public static X509Certificate readX509Certificate(Path path)
        throws CertificateException, IOException {
    CertificateFactory factory =
        CertificateFactory.getInstance("X.509");

    try (InputStream input = Files.newInputStream(path)) {
        return (X509Certificate) factory.generateCertificate(input);
    }
}
```

Inspect:

```java
X509Certificate cert = readX509Certificate(Path.of("server.crt"));

System.out.println(cert.getSubjectX500Principal());
System.out.println(cert.getIssuerX500Principal());
System.out.println(cert.getNotBefore());
System.out.println(cert.getNotAfter());
System.out.println(cert.getSerialNumber());
System.out.println(cert.getSigAlgName());
```

---

# 19. Check Certificate Validity Period

```java
public static void checkValidity(X509Certificate certificate)
        throws CertificateExpiredException, CertificateNotYetValidException {
    certificate.checkValidity();
}
```

At specific time:

```java
certificate.checkValidity(Date.from(instant));
```

Validity checks are necessary but insufficient:

```text
A valid date does not mean certificate is trusted.
You must also validate chain, usage, hostname, and revocation policy if required.
```

---

# 20. Extract SAN DNS Names

```java
public static List<String> dnsSubjectAlternativeNames(
    X509Certificate certificate
) throws CertificateParsingException {
    Collection<List<?>> sans = certificate.getSubjectAlternativeNames();

    if (sans == null) {
        return List.of();
    }

    List<String> result = new ArrayList<>();

    for (List<?> san : sans) {
        Integer type = (Integer) san.get(0);
        Object value = san.get(1);

        // Type 2 = dNSName
        if (type == 2 && value instanceof String dnsName) {
            result.add(dnsName);
        }
    }

    return result;
}
```

Use:

```java
List<String> dnsNames = dnsSubjectAlternativeNames(cert);
```

Rule:

```text
For TLS hostname identity, SAN is the important field.
Fix wrong SAN at certificate issuance time.
Do not bypass hostname verification.
```

---

# 21. Certificate Extensions Cheat Sheet

| Extension | Why It Matters |
|---|---|
| Basic Constraints | says whether cert is CA |
| Key Usage | allowed key operations |
| Extended Key Usage | server auth, client auth, code signing, etc. |
| Subject Alternative Name | DNS/IP/email identities |
| Subject Key Identifier | key identifier |
| Authority Key Identifier | issuer key identifier |
| CRL Distribution Points | where to get CRL |
| Authority Information Access | OCSP / issuer info |
| Certificate Policies | policy OIDs |

Common Extended Key Usage:

| EKU | Meaning |
|---|---|
| `serverAuth` | TLS server certificate |
| `clientAuth` | TLS client certificate |
| `codeSigning` | code signing |
| `emailProtection` | S/MIME |

---

# 22. Programmatic Certificate Path Validation

```java
public static void validateCertificatePath(
    List<X509Certificate> chainLeafFirst,
    Set<TrustAnchor> trustAnchors
) throws GeneralSecurityException {
    CertificateFactory certificateFactory =
        CertificateFactory.getInstance("X.509");

    CertPath certPath =
        certificateFactory.generateCertPath(chainLeafFirst);

    PKIXParameters parameters = new PKIXParameters(trustAnchors);
    parameters.setRevocationEnabled(false);

    CertPathValidator validator =
        CertPathValidator.getInstance("PKIX");

    validator.validate(certPath, parameters);
}
```

Trust anchor:

```java
X509Certificate rootCa = readX509Certificate(Path.of("root-ca.crt"));

TrustAnchor trustAnchor = new TrustAnchor(rootCa, null);

Set<TrustAnchor> anchors = Set.of(trustAnchor);
```

Use case:

```text
offline certificate validation
custom certificate validation utility
B2B message validation
certificate chain diagnostics
```

Warning:

```text
If you disable revocation, you are not checking whether a certificate was revoked.
That may be acceptable for some internal tests, but should be intentional.
```

---

# 23. Revocation: CRL and OCSP

Revocation answers:

```text
Was this certificate cancelled before its expiry date?
```

Mechanisms:

| Mechanism | Meaning |
|---|---|
| CRL | CA publishes revoked cert list |
| OCSP | online status check for specific cert |
| OCSP stapling | server includes OCSP response |

Revocation is operationally hard:

```text
network dependency
CA endpoint availability
soft-fail vs hard-fail decision
latency
caching
internal CA support
firewall/proxy issues
```

Production rule:

```text
Define revocation policy explicitly:
- Is revocation required?
- Is OCSP used?
- Is CRL used?
- Is soft-fail allowed?
- What happens if responder is unavailable?
```

---

# 24. Certificate Identity Mapping

For mTLS, certificate authentication is not enough. You still need application authorization.

Common mappings:

```text
certificate SAN URI -> service identity
certificate SAN DNS -> service name
subject DN -> legacy identity
serial number + issuer -> partner cert record
public key fingerprint -> pinned identity
SPIFFE ID -> workload identity
```

Example conceptual mapping:

```java
public record ClientCertificateIdentity(
    String issuerDn,
    String subjectDn,
    String serialNumber,
    List<String> dnsSans
) {}
```

Rule:

```text
mTLS proves possession of private key for certificate.
Your app still decides what that identity may do.
```

---

# 25. Certificate Fingerprint

```java
public static String sha256Fingerprint(X509Certificate certificate)
        throws GeneralSecurityException, CertificateEncodingException {
    MessageDigest digest = MessageDigest.getInstance("SHA-256");

    byte[] hash = digest.digest(certificate.getEncoded());

    return HexFormat.ofDelimiter(":")
        .withUpperCase()
        .formatHex(hash);
}
```

Use for:

```text
diagnostics
pinning
audit
manual verification
certificate inventory
```

Do not use fingerprint as the only trust model unless you intentionally design pinning and rotation.

---

# 26. Default JVM Truststore

Most Java runtimes include a default CA truststore, often called `cacerts`. Operationally, avoid modifying global `cacerts` unless you own the runtime image and have a repeatable build process.

Better:

```text
application-specific truststore
container image with explicit trust bundle
mounted truststore secret/config
service mesh trust bundle
```

Why:

```text
global mutation is hard to audit
upgrades may overwrite changes
different environments diverge
debugging becomes harder
```

---

# 27. JVM TLS System Properties

Common properties:

```bash
-Djavax.net.ssl.trustStore=/app/certs/truststore.p12
-Djavax.net.ssl.trustStorePassword=changeit
-Djavax.net.ssl.trustStoreType=PKCS12

-Djavax.net.ssl.keyStore=/app/certs/keystore.p12
-Djavax.net.ssl.keyStorePassword=changeit
-Djavax.net.ssl.keyStoreType=PKCS12
```

Use cases:

```text
legacy libraries
application servers
tools that rely on default JSSE context
quick test environment
```

For application code, explicit `SSLContext` is usually clearer.

---

# 28. Debug TLS / PKI Problems

Enable handshake debug:

```bash
-Djavax.net.debug=ssl,handshake
```

Useful for local diagnosis:

```text
which certificate was sent
which truststore was used
why handshake failed
protocol/cipher negotiation
client cert request
certificate chain details
```

Warning:

```text
TLS debug logs can be verbose and may expose sensitive operational data.
Use carefully.
```

---

# 29. Common PKI Failure Messages

| Error | Likely Cause |
|---|---|
| `PKIX path building failed` | CA not trusted or chain incomplete |
| `unable to find valid certification path` | truststore missing CA |
| `No subject alternative DNS name matching ...` | SAN mismatch |
| `CertificateExpiredException` | cert expired |
| `CertificateNotYetValidException` | clock/cert not valid yet |
| `bad_certificate` | peer rejected certificate |
| `handshake_failure` | protocol/cipher/cert mismatch |
| `UnrecoverableKeyException` | wrong key password |
| `Keystore was tampered with` | wrong store password |
| `alias does not exist` | wrong alias |
| `Private key must be accompanied by certificate chain` | bad keystore import |

---

# 30. Diagnostic Commands

## 30.1 Inspect Remote Certificate Chain

```bash
openssl s_client \
  -connect app.internal.example.com:443 \
  -servername app.internal.example.com \
  -showcerts
```

## 30.2 Check Java Trust with Keytool

```bash
keytool -list \
  -v \
  -keystore app-truststore.p12 \
  -storetype PKCS12
```

## 30.3 Print Certificate

```bash
keytool -printcert \
  -file server.crt
```

## 30.4 Check Alias

```bash
keytool -list \
  -alias app-server \
  -keystore app-keystore.p12 \
  -storetype PKCS12
```

---

# 31. PKI in Kubernetes / Cloud / On-Prem

## Kubernetes

```text
certificates may be mounted as Secrets
trust bundle may be injected by service mesh
mTLS may be terminated by sidecar
app may not directly see client cert
rotation may happen by replacing mounted files
```

## AWS / Azure

```text
public cert often terminates at load balancer
private cert may come from private CA
keys may live in KMS / Key Vault / HSM
workload identity may replace app-managed client cert
```

## On-Prem

```text
private CA common
corporate proxy can intercept TLS
custom truststore common
legacy TLS protocol/ciphers common
manual certificate rotation common
```

Senior question:

```text
Where is trust established?
- JVM truststore?
- application SSLContext?
- OS trust?
- service mesh?
- gateway?
- load balancer?
```

---

# 32. Certificate Rotation Pattern

```text
1. Generate new key pair or CSR.
2. CA issues new certificate.
3. Deploy new cert and chain.
4. Keep old trust active during overlap.
5. Verify all clients trust new chain.
6. Switch traffic.
7. Remove old cert after safe window.
8. Update inventory and expiry monitoring.
```

For mTLS:

```text
client cert rotation:
- server truststore must trust new client cert/CA before client switches
- app authorization must recognize new identity

server cert rotation:
- clients must trust new server CA/chain before server switches
```

---

# 33. Expiry Monitoring

Track:

```text
certificate alias
subject
SANs
issuer
serial number
notBefore
notAfter
environment
owner team
usage
rotation runbook
```

Java snippet:

```java
public static long daysUntilExpiry(X509Certificate certificate, Clock clock) {
    Instant expiry = certificate.getNotAfter().toInstant();
    Instant now = clock.instant();

    return Duration.between(now, expiry).toDays();
}
```

Alert thresholds:

```text
90 days
60 days
30 days
14 days
7 days
1 day
expired
```

---

# 34. Certificate Inventory Record

```java
public record CertificateInventoryItem(
    String alias,
    String subject,
    String issuer,
    String serialNumber,
    List<String> dnsSans,
    Instant notBefore,
    Instant notAfter,
    String sha256Fingerprint,
    String environment,
    String owner
) {}
```

Use this for:

```text
dashboard
compliance
rotation planning
incident response
asset management
```

---

# 35. Reading Certificates from KeyStore

```java
public static List<CertificateInventoryItem> inventory(
    KeyStore keyStore,
    Clock clock
) throws GeneralSecurityException {
    List<CertificateInventoryItem> result = new ArrayList<>();

    Enumeration<String> aliases = keyStore.aliases();

    while (aliases.hasMoreElements()) {
        String alias = aliases.nextElement();

        Certificate cert = keyStore.getCertificate(alias);

        if (cert instanceof X509Certificate x509) {
            result.add(new CertificateInventoryItem(
                alias,
                x509.getSubjectX500Principal().getName(),
                x509.getIssuerX500Principal().getName(),
                x509.getSerialNumber().toString(16),
                dnsSubjectAlternativeNames(x509),
                x509.getNotBefore().toInstant(),
                x509.getNotAfter().toInstant(),
                sha256Fingerprint(x509),
                "unknown",
                "unknown"
            ));
        }
    }

    return result;
}
```

---

# 36. CA Hierarchy

Typical enterprise hierarchy:

```text
Offline Root CA
  ↓
Online Intermediate CA
  ↓
Issuing CA
  ↓
Server / Client / User Certificates
```

Why root often offline:

```text
root compromise is catastrophic
root signs intermediates only
intermediates handle day-to-day issuance
rotation/revocation scope is smaller
```

---

# 37. Certificate Policy and CPS

| Document | Meaning |
|---|---|
| CP | Certificate Policy: what rules apply |
| CPS | Certification Practice Statement: how CA operates |
| RA process | registration/identity verification |
| Subscriber agreement | obligations of cert holder |
| Relying party agreement | assumptions for cert consumers |

For internal enterprise PKI, define:

```text
who can request certs
what identities are allowed
key size/algorithm policy
validity period
revocation process
rotation SLA
ownership metadata
audit requirements
```

---

# 38. PKI Security Rules

```text
1. Private keys must never leave secure storage unnecessarily.
2. Root CA private key must be highly protected.
3. Intermediate CA compromise must be revocable.
4. Leaf certificate should have limited validity.
5. Certificate SAN must match actual service name.
6. Truststore must contain CA/trust anchors, not random leaf certs unless pinning.
7. Keystore passwords are secrets.
8. Truststore updates require change control.
9. Revocation policy must be explicit.
10. Certificate rotation must be tested before expiry.
```

NIST SP 800-57 provides general key-management guidance and best practices for cryptographic keying material, including the security services that cryptography can provide and lifecycle management concerns. ([NIST Computer Security Resource Center](https://csrc.nist.gov/pubs/sp/800/57/pt1/r5/final?utm_source=chatgpt.com))

---

# 39. What Should Be in Keystore?

For server identity:

```text
alias: app-server
entry type: PrivateKeyEntry
contains:
- private key
- leaf server certificate
- intermediate chain
```

For client mTLS identity:

```text
alias: app-client
entry type: PrivateKeyEntry
contains:
- private key
- leaf client certificate
- intermediate chain
```

Do not put unrelated trusted CA entries into the same alias as private key.

---

# 40. What Should Be in Truststore?

```text
root CA certificate
intermediate CA certificate, if chosen as trust anchor
partner CA certificate
private CA certificate
specific pinned certificate, only if intentionally pinning
```

Usually avoid:

```text
expired CA
unrelated public CA
random leaf certs without clear reason
duplicate aliases
unknown source certificates
```

---

# 41. Hostname and SAN

For `https://orders.internal.example.com`, certificate should include:

```text
Subject Alternative Name:
DNS: orders.internal.example.com
```

For Kubernetes internal service:

```text
DNS: orders.default.svc.cluster.local
DNS: orders
```

For IP access:

```text
IP: 10.10.1.5
```

Rule:

```text
If client connects by DNS name, cert needs DNS SAN.
If client connects by IP, cert needs IP SAN.
Do not solve SAN mismatch by disabling hostname verification.
```

---

# 42. mTLS Identity Gotcha

Bad assumption:

```text
mTLS success == user is authorized
```

Correct:

```text
mTLS success == peer owns private key for trusted certificate
authorization still must check:
- certificate identity
- mapped principal
- allowed operation
- tenant/scope
- certificate status
```

Example mapping:

```text
SAN URI: spiffe://prod.example.com/ns/order/sa/order-api
maps to:
principal = order-api
environment = prod
permissions = submit-order, read-order
```

---

# 43. Certificate Pinning

Pinning options:

```text
pin exact certificate fingerprint
pin public key fingerprint
pin private CA
```

Prefer, in most enterprise cases:

```text
private CA truststore
short-lived certificates
proper rotation
mTLS
service mesh identity
```

Pinning risks:

```text
rotation outage
emergency CA migration pain
operational lock-in
manual update burden
```

Use only with a rotation story.

---

# 44. Self-Signed Certificates

Self-signed cert is acceptable for:

```text
local development
test environment
internal lab
bootstrap root CA
```

Dangerous if:

```text
used as leaf cert in production without trust governance
shared private key copied everywhere
imported blindly into global cacerts
no expiry monitoring
no rotation process
```

Better internal model:

```text
create private root/intermediate CA
issue leaf certs from it
trust the CA, not every individual leaf
```

---

# 45. Public CA vs Private CA

| CA Type | Use |
|---|---|
| Public CA | browser/public internet TLS |
| Private CA | internal services, mTLS, private domains |
| Partner CA | B2B trust |
| Self-signed root | internal CA bootstrap |
| Self-signed leaf | dev/test only |

Rule:

```text
Public-facing browser/API endpoint usually needs public CA.
Internal service identity usually uses private CA or service mesh identity.
```

---

# 46. Key Algorithm Choices

Common practical choices:

| Purpose | Algorithm |
|---|---|
| TLS server cert | RSA 2048/3072 or ECDSA P-256/P-384 |
| Client mTLS cert | RSA/ECDSA depending ecosystem |
| Signing modern app messages | Ed25519 / ECDSA / RSA-PSS |
| Legacy compatibility | RSA |
| Smaller cert/signature | ECDSA / Ed25519 |
| Broad old-system compatibility | RSA |

Always align with:

```text
organization crypto policy
JDK support
provider support
partner compatibility
FIPS/compliance constraints
```

---

# 47. PKI and Java Module / Provider Awareness

Java crypto/security is provider-based. Algorithm availability can vary by JDK version, provider, policy, and runtime distribution.

Check providers:

```java
for (Provider provider : Security.getProviders()) {
    System.out.println(provider.getName());
}
```

Check algorithms:

```java
Set<String> algorithms = Security.getAlgorithms("Signature");

algorithms.stream()
    .sorted()
    .forEach(System.out::println);
```

Rule:

```text
Do not assume every algorithm is available in every runtime/provider.
Test in the same JDK/container image used in production.
```

---

# 48. Common Anti-Patterns

| Anti-Pattern | Risk |
|---|---|
| Import partner cert into global `cacerts` manually | untracked drift |
| Disable hostname verification | MITM risk |
| Trust-all `TrustManager` | no real security |
| Self-signed leaf cert in production | no manageable trust chain |
| Expired certificate discovered by outage | missing monitoring |
